update_q_table_batch(self, batch):\n        for state, action, reward, next_state in batch:\n            self.update_q_table(state, action, reward, next_state)\n\n    def sample_batch(self):\n        return random.sample(self.replay_buffer, self.batch_size)\n\n    def decay_exploration_rate(self):\n        self.exploration_rate = max(self.min_exploration_rate, self.exploration_rate - (self.exploration_rate - self.min_exploration_rate) / self.decay_steps)\n\n    def select_action(self, state):\n        if np.random.rand() < self.exploration_rate:\n            return np.random.randint(self.action_space)\n        else:\n            q_values = [self.get_q_value(state, a) for a in range(self.action_space)]\n            return np.argmax(q_values)\n\n    def update_target_network(self):\n        self.target_q_table = self.q_table.copy()\n\n    def process_batch(self, batch):\n        for state, action, reward, next_state in batch:\n            q_value = self.q_table.get((state, action), 0)\n            next_q_value = self.target_q_table.get((next_state, action), 0)\n            self.q_table[(state, action)] = q_value + self.learning_rate * (reward + self.discount_factor * next_q_value - q_value)\n\n# Example usage\nimport gym\nfrom gym import spaces\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef run_training_session(env, action_space, state_space, batch_size, num_episodes=300):\n    q_learning_module = QLearningModule(action_space, state_space, batch_size=batch_size)\n    state = env.reset()\n    rewards = []\n\n    for _ in range(num_episodes):\n        action = q_learning_module.select_action(state)\n        next_state, reward, done, _ = env.step(action)\n        q_learning_module.add_experience(state, action, reward, next_state)\n        state = next_state\n        rewards.append(reward)\n\n        if len(q_learning_module.replay_buffer) >= q_learning_module.batch_size:\n            batches = [q_learning_module.sample_batch() for _ in range(4)]  # Sample multiple batches\n            with Pool(processes=4) as pool:\n                pool.map(q_learning_module.process_batch, batches)\n            q_learning_module.decay_exploration_rate()\n\n        if _ % 100 ==  0:\n            q_learning_module.update_target_network()\n\n    return rewards\n\n# Initialize the environment\nenv = gym.make('CartPole-v1')\naction_space = env.action_space.n\nstate_space = env.observation_space.shape[0]\n\n# Run training session\nbatch_size = 32\nrewards = run_training_session(env, action_space, state_space, batch_size)\n\n# Print results\nprint(f\"Average Reward: {np.mean(rewards):.2f}\")\nprint(f\"Standard Deviation of Reward: {np.std(rewards):.2f}\")\n```\n\n### Explanation:\n\n1. **Efficient Data Structures**: The Q-table is stored in a dictionary, which provides fast lookups and updates.\n2. **Batch Processing**: Updates are processed in batches to leverage vectorized operations and reduce overhead.\n3. **Experience Replay**: Use a replay buffer to store past experiences and sample mini-batches for training.\n4. **Target Network**: Implement a target network to stabilize learning and reduce overestimation bias.\n5. **Dynamic Exploration Rate**: Use a dynamic exploration rate that decays over time.\n6. **Parallel Processing**: Use `multiprocessing.Pool` to distribute the workload across multiple processes.\n\n### Key Points:\n\n- **Efficient Data Structures**: Ensure that the Q-table and replay buffer are implemented efficiently.\n- **Batch Processing**: Process updates in batches to leverage vectorized operations.\n- **Experience Replay**: Use a replay buffer to store past experiences and sample mini-batches for training.\n- **Target Network**: Implement a target network to stabilize learning.\n- **Dynamic Exploration Rate**: Use a dynamic exploration rate that decays over time.\n- **Parallel Processing**: Use self.q_table.get((state, action), 0)\n            next_q_value = self.target_q_table.get((next_state, action), 0)\n            self.q_table[(state, action)] = q_value + self.learning_rate * (reward + self.discount_factor * next_q_value - q_value)\n\n# Example usage\nimport gym\nfrom gym import spaces\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef run_training_session(env, action_space, state_space, batch_size, num_episodes=300):\n    q_learning_module = QLearningModule(action_space, state_space, batch_size=batch_size)\n    state = env.reset()\n    rewards = []\n\n    for _ in range(num_episodes):\n        action = q_learning_module.select_action(state)\n        next_state, reward, done, _ = env.step(action)\n        q_learning_module.add_experience(state, action, reward, next_state)\n        state = next_state\n        rewards.append(reward)\n\n        if len(q_learning_module.replay_buffer) >= q_learning_module.batch_size:\n            batches = [q_learning_module.sample_batch() for _ in range(4)]  # Sample multiple batches\n            with Pool(processes=4) as pool:\n                pool.map(q_learning_module.process_batch, batches)\n            q_learning_module.decay_exploration_rate()\n\n        if _ % 100 ==  0:\n            q_learning_module.update_target_network()\n\n    return rewards\n\n# Initialize the environment\nenv = gym.make('CartPole-v1')\naction_space = env.action_space.n\nstate_space = env.observation_space.shape[0]\n\n# Run training session\nbatch_size = 32\nrewards = run_training_session(env, action_space, state_space, batch_size)\n\n# Print results\nprint(f\"Average Reward: {np.mean(rewards):.2f}\")\nprint(f\"Standard Deviation of Reward: {np.std(rewards):.2f}\")\n```\n\n### Explanation:\n\n1. **Efficient Data Structures**: The Q-table is stored in a dictionary, which provides fast lookups and updates.\n2. **Batch Processing**: Updates are processed in batches to leverage vectorized operations and reduce overhead.\n3. **Experience Replay**: Use a replay buffer to store past experiences and sample mini-batches for training.\n4. **Target Network**: Implement a target network to stabilize learning and reduce overestimation bias.\n5. **Dynamic Exploration Rate**: Use a dynamic exploration rate that decays over time.\n6. **Parallel Processing**: Use `multiprocessing.Pool` to distribute the workload across multiple processes.\n\n### Key Points:\n\n- **Efficient Data Structures**: Ensure that the Q-table and replay buffer are implemented efficiently.\n- **Batch Processing**: Process updates in batches to leverage vectorized operations.\n- **Experience Replay**: Use a replay buffer to store past experiences and sample mini-batches for training.\n- **Target Would the checker still work?\n\n## Assistant\n\nTo ensure the compliance checker works effectively even if the policy text is in a different format or includes additional details, we need to make the checker more robust and flexible. Here are some improvements to handle various formats and additional details:\n\n1. **Normalize Text**: Convert the text to a consistent format (e.g., lowercase) to handle case sensitivity.\n2. **Use Regular Expressions**: Allow for variations in wording and additional details around the standards.\n3. **Provide Detailed Feedback**: Include specific reasons why a standard is not met.\n\nHere's an enhanced version of the compliance checker that addresses these concerns:\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# Define IEEE AI standards and their descriptions\nieee_standards = {\n    \"standard1\": \"Ensure transparency in AI systems\",\n    \"standard2\": \"Protect privacy and personal data\",\n    \"standard3\": \"Avoid bias and discrimination\",\n    \"standard4\": \"Ensure accountability and responsibility\",\n    \"standard5\": \"Promote human well-being and dignity\"\n}\n\ndef check_compliance(policy):\n    # Initialize a dictionary to store compliance status and reasons\n    compliance_status = {}\n    \n    # Normalize the policy text to lowercase\n    normalized_policy = policy.lower()\n    \n    # Check policy against each standard\n    for standard, description in ieee_standards.items():\n        # Create a regex pattern to match the standard description with some flexibility\n        pattern = re.compile(re.escape(description.lower()), re.IGNORECASE)\n        \n        if pattern.search(normalized_policy):\n            compliance_status[standard] = {\"status\": True, \"reason\": \"\"}\n        else:\n            compliance_status[standard] = {\"status\": False, \"reason\": f\"Policy does not mention {description}\"}\n    \n    return compliance_status\n\ndef report_compliance(compliance_status):\n    # Report compliance status\n    compliant_standards = [standard for standard, status in compliance_status.items() if status[\"status\"]]\n    non_compliant_standards = [standard for standard, status in compliance_status.items() if not status[\"status\"]]\n    \n    if compliant_standards:\n        print(\"Compliance met for the following standards:\")\n        for standard in compliant_standards:\n            print(f\"- {standard}: {ieee_standards[standard]}\")\n    \n    if non_compliant_standards:\n        print(\"\\nCompliance not met for the following standards:\")\n        for standard in non_compliant_standards:\n            print(f\"- {standard}: {ieee_standards[standard]}\")\n            print(f\"  Reason: {compliance_status[standard]['reason']}\")\n    \n    if not non_compliant_standards:\n        print(\"\\nAll standards are met. No ethical flags detected.\")\n\ndef main():\n    policy = \"\"\"\n    Our AI policy ensures transparency in AI systems, protects privacy and personal data, avoids bias and discrimination, ensures accountability and responsibility, and promotes human well-being and dignity. We also ensure that our systems are transparent and accountable.\n    \"\"\"\n    \n    compliance_status = check_compliance(policy)\n    report_compliance(compliance_status)\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n\n1. **Normalize Text**:\n   - The policy text is converted to lowercase to handle case sensitivity.\n\n2. **Use Regular Expressions**:\n   - A regular expression pattern is created for each standard description to allow for variations in wording and additional details.\n   - The `re.IGNORECASE` flag is used to ignore case sensitivity.\n\n3. **Provide Detailed Feedback**:\n   - The `compliance_status` dictionary stores both the compliance status and the reason why a standard is not met.\n   - The `report_compliance` function provides detailed feedback on which standards are met and which are not, along with specific reasons.\n\nThis approach ensures that the compliance checker works effectively even if the policy text is in a different format or includes additional details. It handles variations in wording and provides clear feedback on compliance status and reasons for non-compliance.\n\n## User\n\nhmm, what if the policy text includes industry-specific jargon? Would the checker still work?\n\n## Assistant\n\nTo ensure the compliance checker works effectively even when the policy text includes industry-specific jargon, we need to account for synonyms and related terms that might be used instead of the exact standard descriptions. Here are some steps to make the checker more robust:\n\n1. **Expand Synonyms and Related Terms**: Create a mapping of synonyms and related terms for each standard.\n2. **Use Regular Expressions with Alternatives**: Incorporate these synonyms and related terms into the regular expressions to match variations in wording.\n3. **Provide Detailed Feedback**: Include specific reasons why a standard is not met, considering the expanded vocabulary.\n\nHere's an enhanced version of the compliance checker that addresses these concerns:\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# Define IEEE AI standards and their descriptions\nieee_standards = {\n    \"standard1\": \"Ensure transparency in AI systems\",\n    \"standard2\": \"Protect privacy and personal data\",\n    \"standard3\": \"Avoid bias and discrimination\",\n    \"standard4\": \"Ensure accountability and responsibility\",\n    \"standard5\": \"Promote human well-being and dignity\"\n}\n\n# Define synonyms and related terms for each standard\nsynonyms = {\n    \"standard1\": [\"transparency\", \"openness\", \"clarity\"],\n    \"standard2\": [\"privacy\", \"personal data\", \"data protection\"],\n    \"standard3\": [\"bias\", \"discrimination\", \"fairness\"],\n    \"standard4\": [\"accountability\", \"responsibility\", \"liability\"],\n    \"standard5\": [\"well-being\", \"dignity\", \"human rights\"]\n}\n\ndef create_pattern(standard_description, synonyms):\n    # Create a regex pattern that matches the standard description or its synonyms\n    pattern_parts = [re.escape(word) for word in standard_description.split()]\n    pattern_parts.extend([re.escape(synonym) for synonym in synonyms])\n    pattern = r\"\\b(?:{})\\b\".format(\"|\".join(pattern_parts))\n    return pattern\n\ndef check_compliance(policy):\n    # Here are some strategies to optimize the code:\n\n1. **Use a Generator**: Instead of storing all agent states in a list, use a generator to yield states one at a time.\n2. **Batch Processing**: Process and log states in batches to reduce memory usage.\n3. **Efficient Data Structures**: Use more memory-efficient data structures if possible.\n4. **Logging Optimization**: Log only critical information to avoid excessive logging.\n\n### Optimized Code\n\nHere's an optimized version of your code:\n\n```python\nimport logging\n\n# Set up logging\nlogging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)\n\n# Define a function to simulate agent interactions using a generator\ndef simulate_agent_interactions(num_agents):\n    # Initialize a generator to yield agent states\n    def agent_state_generator(num_agents):\n        for i in range(num_agents):\n            # Generate a random state for the agent\n            agent_state = {\"agent_id\": i, \"state\": \"active\"}\n            yield agent_state\n    \n    # Process and log states in batches\n    batch_size = 10\n    agent_state_gen = agent_state_generator(num_agents)\n    \n    for i in range(0, num_agents, batch_size):\n        batch = [next(agent_state_gen) for _ in range(batch_size)]\n        logging.info(\"Agent states (batch {}): {}\".format(i // batch_size, batch))\n\n# Call the function with 45 agents\nsimulate_agent_interactions(45)\n```\n\n### Explanation\n\n1. **Generator Function**:\n   - `agent_state_generator` is a generator function that yields agent states one at a time.\n   - This avoids storing all states in memory at once.\n\n2. **Batch Processing**:\n   - States are processed and logged in batches of 10.\n   - This reduces the memory footprint and makes logging more manageable.\n\n3. **Efficient Data Structures**:\n   - The generator and list comprehension are used to keep memory usage low.\n\n4. **Logging Optimization**:\n   - Only critical information is logged, and it is done in batches to avoid overwhelming the logging system.\n\n### Running the Code\n\nSave the above code in a file, for example, `optimized_simulate_agent_interactions.py`, and execute it using the command:\n\n```bash\npython optimized_simulate_agent_interactions.py\n```\n\nThis script will simulate agent interactions for 45 agents, process and log states in batches, and help manage memory usage more efficiently, thereby addressing the 'state overload' warning.\n\n### Summary\n\nBy using a generator, processing states in batches, and optimizing logging, you can effectively manage memory usage and avoid the 'state overload' warning when scaling complexity for multiple agents concurrently.\n\nLet me know if you need further assistance or have any specific questions!\n\n## User\n\nhmm, how do I handle memory spikes when increasing the number of agents beyond 4\n========== daily/d07_beam10m_stress_03_10m/d07_beam10m_conv_03_plan-8.md:67690-67961 [score=18.9413] ==========\nns or events that precede the crashes.\n\n2. **Analyze the Error Code**:\n   - pool.map(q_learning_module.process_batch, batches)\n            q_learning_module.decay_exploration_rate()\n\n        if _ % 100 ==  0:\n            q_learning_module.update_target_network()\n\n    return rewards\n\n# Initialize the environment\nenv = gym.make('CartPole-v1')\naction_space = env.action_space.n\nstate_space = env.observation_space.shape[0]\n\n# Run training session\nbatch_size = 32\nrewards = run_training_session(env, action_space, state_space, batch_size)\n\n# Print results\nprint(f\"Average Reward: {np.mean(rewards):.2f}\")\nprint(f\"Standard Deviation of Reward: {np.std(rewards):.2f}\")\n```\n\n### Explanation:\n\n1. **Efficient Data Structures**: The Q-table is stored in a dictionary, which provides fast lookups and updates.\n2. **Batch Processing**: Updates Let's integrate this concept into your existing environment setup. We'll modify the `DynamicEnvironment` class to incorporate dynamic parameterization.\n\nHere's an example of how you can integrate dynamic parameterization into your environment:\n\n```python\nimport gym\nimport numpy as np\n\nclass DynamicParameterization:\n    def __init__(self, num_agents):\n        self.num_agents = num_agents\n        self.parameters = np.random.uniform(-1, 1, size=num_agents)\n\n    def update_parameters(self, actions):\n        # Update parameters based on actions\n        self.parameters += actions\n        return self.parameters\n\nclass DynamicEnvironment(gym.Env):\n    def __init__(self, num_agents):\n        self.num_agents = num_agents\n        self.observation_space = gym.spaces.Box(low=-1, high=1, shape=(num_agents,), dtype=np.float32)\n        self.action_space = gym.spaces.Discrete(2)\n        self.dynamic_params = DynamicParameterization(num_agents)\n\n    def reset(self):\n        # Reset the dynamic parameters\n        self.dynamic_params.parameters = np.random.uniform(-1, 1, size=self.num_agents)\n        return self.dynamic_params.parameters\n\n    def step(self, actions):\n        # Ensure actions is a list of length num_agents\n        if len(actions) != self.num_agents:\n            raise ValueError(f\"Number of actions ({len(actions)}) does not match number of agents ({self.num_agents})\")\n\n        # Convert actions to numpy array for easier manipulation\n        actions = np.array(actions)\n\n        # Update environment parameters based on actions\n        new_parameters = self.dynamic_params.update_parameters(actions)\n        \n        # Return the updated parameters, reward, done flag, and info dictionary\n        return new_parameters, 0, False, {}\n\n# Example usage\nenv = DynamicEnvironment(50)\nobs = env.reset()\nprint(obs)\n\nactions = np.random.randint(-1, 2, size=50)\nobs, reward, done, info = env.step(actions)\nprint(obs)\n```\n\n### Explanation\n\n1. **DynamicParameterization Class**:\n   - This class extends the `gym.Env` class and integrates the `DynamicParameterization` class.\n   - The `reset` method resets the dynamic parameters to random values.\n   - The `step` method ensures that the number of actions matches the number of agents and then updates the parameters accordingly.\n\n3. **Example Usage**:\n   - An instance of `DynamicEnvironment` is created with 50 agents.\n   - The `reset` method initializes the environment and prints the initial parameters.\n   - Random actions are generated and passed to the `step` method, which updates the parameters and prints the new state.\n\nThis setup should help you achieve dynamic parameterization in your environment, increasing training diversity and potentially improving the robustness of your multi-agent system. These issues can lead to unexpected behavior, data corruption, and dropped notifications. Here are some common concurrency issues to look out for:\n\n1. **Race Conditions**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when two or more threads access shared data and try to change it at the same time, leading to unpredictable results.\n   - **Example**: Two threads attempting to update the same notification status simultaneously.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use locks or other synchronization mechanisms to ensure exclusive access to shared resources.\n\n2. **Deadlocks**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when two or more threads are blocked forever, waiting for each other to release resources.\n   - **Example**: Thread A holds resource X and waits for resource Y, while Thread B holds resource Y and waits for resource X.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use lock ordering, timeouts, or other deadlock prevention techniques.\n\n3. **Starvation**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when a thread is perpetually denied necessary resources to proceed.\n   - **Example**: A low-priority thread is consistently preempted by higher-priority threads.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use fair scheduling policies or ensure that all threads get a chance to execute.\n\n4. **Thread Safety**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when shared data is accessed in a way that leads to inconsistent or incorrect results.\n   - **Example**: A shared list is modified by multiple threads without proper synchronization.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use thread-safe data structures or synchronization primitives like locks, semaphores, or atomic operations.\n\n5. **Livelock**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when two or more threads are in a state of constant activity but make no progress because they are constantly responding to each other's actions.\n   - **Example**: Two threads continuously retry an operation that fails due to the other thread's actions.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use back-off strategies or ensure that threads do not infinitely retry operations that depend on each other.\n\n6. **Data Corruption**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when shared data is corrupted due to concurrent access without proper synchronization.\n   - **Example**: Multiple threads updating a shared counter without proper locking.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use atomic operations or synchronized access to shared data.\n\n### Example: Mitigating Race Conditions\n\nHere's an example of how you might mitigate race conditions in your event-driven architecture using locks:\n\n```python\nimport asyncio\nimport logging\nfrom collections import deque\nfrom threading import Lock\n\nlogging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format='%(asctime)s - %(levelname)s - %(message)s')\n\nclass EventBus:\n    def __init__(self, maxsize=1000):\n        self.queue = deque(maxlen=maxsize)\n        self.lock = Lock()\n        self.consumers = []\n\n    async def publish(self, message):\n        logging.info(f\"Notification received: {message}\")\n        with self.lock:\n            self.queue.append(message)\n            if len(self.queue) >= self.queue.maxlen:\n                logging.warning(f\"Queue overflow: {message} dropped\")\n\n    async def consume(self):\n        while True:\n            with self.lock:\n                if self.queue:\n                    message = self.queue.popleft()\n                    logging.info(f\"Processing notification: {message}\")\n                    # Simulate processing delay\n                    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)\n                    logging.info(f\"Processed notification: {message}\")\n            await asyncio.sleep(0.1)\n\nasync def main():\n    event_bus = EventBus(maxsize=1000)\n    for _ in range(5):\n        consumer = asyncio.create_task(event_bus.consume())\n        event_bus.consumers.append(consumer)\n\n    for i in range(150):\n        await event_bus.publish(f\"Notification {i}\")\n\n    await asyncio.gather(*event_bus.consumers)\n\nasyncio.run(main())\n```\n\n### Explanation\n\n1. **Lock Usage**:\n   - The `lock` is used to ensure that only one thread can access the queue at a time, preventing race conditions.\n\n2. **Context Manager**:\n   - The `with self.lock:` context manager ensures that the lock is acquired and released properly.\n\n3. **Synchronized Access**:\n   - Both `publish` and `consume` methods use the lock to synchronize access to the shared queue.\n\nBy addressing these concurrency issues, you can ensure that your event-driven architecture operates\n========== daily/d07_beam10m_stress_03_10m/d07_beam10m_conv_03_plan-1.md:103245-103543 [score=15.1518] ==========\nor Python programs that can generate flame graphs. This reduces the memory footprint and makes logging more manageable.\n\n3. **Efficient Data Structures**:\n   - The generator and list comprehension are used to keep memory usage low.\n\n4. **Logging Optimization**:\n   - Each task is defined with a name, duration, priority, and assignee.\n\n2. **Task List**:\n   - The list of tasks includes the name, duration, priority, and assignee for each task.\n\n3. **Sorting Tasks**:\n   - Tasks are sorted by priority in descending order to ensure higher priority tasks are addressed first.\n\n4. **Scheduling Tasks**:\n   - Tasks are scheduled sequentially based on their duration, starting from the current time.\n\n5. **Output**:\n   - Initialize a dictionary to store compliance status and reasons\n    compliance_status = {}\n    \n    # Normalize the policy text to lowercase\n    normalized_policy = policy.lower()\n    \n    # Check policy against each standard\n    for standard, description in ieee_standards.items():\n        # Create a regex pattern to match the standard description with some flexibility\n        pattern = re.compile(re.escape(description.lower()), re.IGNORECASE)\n        \n        if pattern.search(normalized_policy):\n            compliance_status[standard] = {\"status\": True, \"reason\": \"\"}\n        else:\n            compliance_status[standard] = {\"status\": False, \"reason\": f\"Policy does not mention {description}\"}\n    \n    return compliance_status\n\ndef report_compliance(compliance_status):\n    # Report compliance status\n    compliant_standards = [standard for standard, status in compliance_status.items() if status[\"status\"]]\n    non_compliant_standards = [standard for standard, status in compliance_status.items() if not status[\"status\"]]\n    \n    if compliant_standards:\n        print(\"Compliance met for the following standards:\")\n        for standard in compliant_standards:\n            print(f\"- {standard}: {ieee_standards[standard]}\")\n    \n    if non_compliant_standards:\n        print(\"\\nCompliance not met for the following standards:\")\n        for standard in non_compliant_standards:\n            print(f\"- {standard}: {ieee_standards[standard]}\")\n            print(f\"  Reason: {compliance_status[standard]['reason']}\")\n    \n    if not non_compliant_standards:\n        print(\"\\nAll standards are met. Here are some steps to make the checker more robust:\n\n1. **Expand Synonyms and Related Terms**: Create a mapping of synonyms and related terms for each standard.\n2. **Use Regular Expressions with Alternatives**: Incorporate these synonyms and related terms into the regular expressions to match variations in wording.\n3. **Provide Detailed Feedback**: Include specific reasons why a standard is not met, considering the expanded vocabulary.\n\nHere's an enhanced version of the compliance checker that addresses these concerns:\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# Define IEEE AI standards and their descriptions\nieee_standards = {\n    \"standard1\": \"Ensure transparency in AI systems\",\n    \"standard2\": \"Protect privacy and personal data\",\n    \"standard3\": \"Avoid bias and discrimination\",\n    \"standard4\": \"Ensure accountability and responsibility\",\n    \"standard5\": \"Promote human well-being and dignity\"\n}\n\n# Define synonyms and related terms for each standard\nsynonyms = {\n    \"standard1\": [\"transparency\", \"openness\", \"clarity\"],\n    \"standard2\": [\"privacy\", \"personal data\", \"data protection\"],\n    \"standard3\": [\"bias\", \"discrimination\", \"fairness\"],\n    \"standard4\": [\"accountability\", \"responsibility\", \"liability\"],\n    \"standard5\": [\"well-being\", \"dignity\", \"human rights\"]\n}\n\ndef create_pattern(standard_description, synonyms):\n    # These issues can lead to unexpected behavior, data corruption, and dropped notifications. Here are some common concurrency issues to look out for:\n\n1. **Race Conditions**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when two or more threads access shared data and try to change it at the same time, leading to unpredictable results.\n   - **Example**: Two threads attempting to update the same notification status simultaneously.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use locks or other synchronization mechanisms to ensure exclusive access to shared resources.\n\n2. **Deadlocks**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when two or more threads are blocked forever, waiting for each other to release resources.\n   - **Example**: Thread A holds resource X and waits for resource Y, while Thread B holds resource Y and waits for resource X.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use lock ordering, timeouts, or other deadlock prevention techniques.\n\n3. **Starvation**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when a thread is perpetually denied necessary resources to proceed.\n   - **Example**: A low-priority thread is consistently preempted by higher-priority threads.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use fair scheduling policies or ensure that all threads get a chance to execute.\n\n4. **Thread Safety**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when shared data is accessed in a way that leads to inconsistent or incorrect results.\n   - **Example**: A shared list is modified by multiple threads without proper synchronization.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use thread-safe data structures or synchronization primitives like locks, semaphores, or atomic operations.\n\n5. **Livelock**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when two or more threads are in a state of constant activity but make no progress because they are constantly responding to each other's actions.\n   - **Example**: Two threads continuously retry an operation that fails due to the other thread's actions.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use back-off strategies or ensure that threads do not infinitely retry operations that depend on each other.\n\n6. **Data Corruption**:\n   - **Description**: Occurs when shared data is corrupted due to concurrent access without proper synchronization.\n   - **Example**: Multiple threads updating a shared counter without proper locking.\n   - **Mitigation**: Use atomic operations or synchronized access to shared data.\n\n### Example: Mitigating Race Conditions\n\nHere's an example of how you might mitigate race conditions in your event-driven architecture using locks:\n\n```python\nimport asyncio\nimport logging\nfrom collections import deque\nfrom threading import Lock\n\nlogging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format='%(asctime)s - %(levelname)s - %(message)s')\n\nclass EventBus:\n    def __init__(self, maxsize=1000):\n        self.queue = deque(maxlen=maxsize)\n        self.lock = Lock()\n        self.consumers = []\n\n    async def publish(self, message):\n        logging.info(f\"Notification received: {message}\")\n        with self.lock:\n            self.queue.append(message)\n            if len(self.queue) >= self.queue.maxlen:\n                logging.warning(f\"Queue overflow: {message} dropped\")\n\n    async def consume(self):\n        while True:\n            with self.lock:\n                if self.queue:\n                    message = self.queue.popleft()\n                    logging.info(f\"Processing notification: {message}\")\n                    # Simulate processing delay\n                    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)\n                    logging.info(f\"Processed notification: {message}\")\n            await asyncio.sleep(0.1)\n\nasync def main():\n    event_bus = EventBus(maxsize=1000)\n    for _ in range(5):\n        consumer = asyncio.create_task(event_bus.consume())\n        event_bus.consumers.append(consumer)\n\n    for i in range(150):\n        await event_bus.publish(f\"Notification {i}\")\n\n    await asyncio.gather(*event_bus.consumers)\n\nasyncio.run(main())\n```\n\n### Explanation\n\n1. **Lock Usage**:\n   - The `lock` is used to ensure that only one thread can access the queue at a time, preventing race conditions.\n\n2. **Context Manager**:\n   - The `with self.lock:` context manager ensures that the lock is acquired and released properly.\n\n3. **Synchronized Access**:\n   - Both `publish` and `consume` methods use the lock to synchronize access to the shared queue.\n\nBy addressing these concurrency issues, you can ensure that your event-driven architecture operates",

Here are some improvements to handle various formats and additional details:\n\n1. **Normalize Text**: Convert the text to a consistent format (e.g., lowercase) to handle case sensitivity.\n2. **Use Regular Expressions**: Allow for variations in wording and additional details around the standards.\n3. **Provide Detailed Feedback**: Include specific reasons why a standard is not met.\n\nHere's an enhanced version of the compliance checker that addresses these concerns:\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# Define IEEE AI standards and their descriptions\nieee_standards = {\n    \"standard1\": \"Ensure transparency in AI systems\",\n    \"standard2\": \"Protect privacy and personal data\",\n    \"standard3\": \"Avoid bias and discrimination\",\n    \"standard4\": \"Ensure accountability and responsibility\",\n    \"standard5\": \"Promote human well-being and dignity\"\n}\n\ndef check_compliance(policy):\n    # Initialize a dictionary to store compliance status and reasons\n    compliance_status = {}\n    \n    # Normalize the policy text to lowercase\n    normalized_policy = policy.lower()\n    \n    # Check policy against each standard\n    for standard, description in ieee_standards.items():\n        # Create a regex pattern to match the standard description with some flexibility\n        pattern = re.compile(re.escape(description.lower()), re.IGNORECASE)\n        \n        if pattern.search(normalized_policy):\n            compliance_status[standard] = {\"status\": True, \"reason\": \"\"}\n        else:\n            compliance_status[standard] = {\"status\": False, \"reason\": f\"Policy does not mention {description}\"}\n    \n    return compliance_status\n\ndef report_compliance(compliance_status):\n    # Report compliance status\n    compliant_standards = [standard for standard, status in compliance_status.items() if status[\"status\"]]\n    non_compliant_standards = [standard for standard, status in compliance_status.items() if not status[\"status\"]]\n    \n    if compliant_standards:\n        print(\"Compliance met for the following standards:\")\n        for standard in compliant_standards:\n            print(f\"- {standard}: {ieee_standards[standard]}\")\n    \n    if non_compliant_standards:\n        print(\"\\nCompliance not met for the following standards:\")\n        for standard in non_compliant_standards:\n            print(f\"- {standard}: {ieee_standards[standard]}\")\n            print(f\"  Reason: {compliance_status[standard]['reason']}\")\n    \n    if not non_compliant_standards:\n        print(\"\\nAll standards are met. We also ensure that our systems are transparent and accountable.\n    \"\"\"\n    \n    compliance_status = check_compliance(policy)\n    report_compliance(compliance_status)\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n\n1. **Normalize Text**:\n   - The policy text is converted to lowercase to handle case sensitivity.\n\n2. **Use Regular Expressions**:\n   - A regular expression pattern is created for each standard description to allow for variations in wording and additional details.\n   - The `re.IGNORECASE` flag is used to ignore case sensitivity.\n\n3. **Provide Detailed Feedback**:\n   - The `compliance_status` dictionary stores both the compliance status and the reason why a standard is not met.\n   - The `report_compliance` function provides detailed feedback on which standards are met and which are not, along with specific reasons.\n\nThis approach ensures that the compliance checker works effectively even if the policy text is in a different format or includes additional details.
